And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. That crooked, swollen side on the videos — once I notice it, I just feel like they’re all staring at that instead of hearing what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people staring at you if you didn't notice the crooked or swollen appearance on the videos?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much. Then I can’t get it out of my head, and I just assume everyone’s staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel just as anxious about teaching if you could somehow ensure that nobody noticed your face, but everything else about your teaching remained the same?\n\nPatient: I think… no, probably not just as anxious. If I somehow knew nobody could notice my face, the teaching itself might be okay. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. That one side… it looks crooked, kind of swollen on videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone’s looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance happen every time you see yourself in videos or is it something that comes and goes?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much whenever I see myself in those videos. Once I catch that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I can’t really unsee it. Then I start thinking everyone must be noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance bother you when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Mostly when other people might see it, yeah. Even if I’m alone watching the lesson video, once I notice that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I start thinking the students or parents or staff must be staring at it. It’s hard to get past that.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face's appearance affected your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, but I’ve fallen behind on a lot of things—lesson planning, mentoring, even basketball coaching stuff. I just don’t get things finished on time anymore.\n\nAdmin has started writing up missed deadlines and coverage issues, so… I’m pretty worried I might lose my position if it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen, or is it just that you feel like it appears that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it does look that way. Like one side of my face looks crooked, kind of swollen, especially on the lesson videos. And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I feel like the students, parents, staff… they’re all staring at that instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your face actually appears crooked or swollen to others when they look at you in person, not just in videos?\n\nPatient: I… I think it does, yes. Like they’re staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see your face as crooked or swollen when they look at you, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I think they do see it. I know maybe that sounds… I don’t know. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone’s looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance happen every time you see yourself in videos or is it something that comes and goes?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much whenever I see myself in those videos. Once I catch that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I can’t really unsee it. Then I start thinking everyone must be noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance bother you when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Mostly when other people might see it, yeah. Even if I’m alone watching the lesson video, once I notice that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I start thinking the students or parents or staff must be staring at it. It’s hard to get past that.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face's appearance affected your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, but I’ve fallen behind on a lot of things—lesson planning, mentoring, even basketball coaching stuff. I just don’t get things finished on time anymore.\n\nAdmin has started writing up missed deadlines and coverage issues, so… I’m pretty worried I might lose my position if it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen, or is it just that you feel like it appears that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it does look that way. Like one side of my face looks crooked, kind of swollen, especially on the lesson videos. And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. Or… how I see it, anyway. That crooked, swollen side on the videos — once I notice it, I just feel like they’re all staring at that instead of hearing what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people staring at you if you didn't notice the crooked or swollen appearance on the videos?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much. It really starts when I see it on the lesson videos… that crooked, swollen-looking side. Then I can’t get it out of my head, and I just assume everyone’s staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel just as anxious about teaching if you could somehow ensure that nobody noticed your face, but everything else about your teaching remained the same?\n\nPatient: I think… no, probably not just as anxious. If I somehow knew nobody could notice my face, the teaching itself might be okay. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I feel like the students, parents, staff… they’re all staring at that instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your face actually appears crooked or swollen to others when they look at you in person, not just in videos?\n\nPatient: I… I think it does, yes. Or at least I can’t shake that it does. When I see that crooked, swollen-looking side in the lesson videos, it feels like that must be what everyone sees in person too… students, parents, staff. Like they’re staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see your face as crooked or swollen when they look at you, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I think they do see it. I know maybe that sounds… I don’t know. But when I see that crooked, swollen side in the lesson videos, it feels like that’s what everyone must be looking at — the students, parents, staff — not what I’m actually saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that nobody else noticed or cared about the way your face looks?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. Even if no one noticed, I’d still see it. That crooked, swollen-looking side in the videos… I can’t stop focusing on it. But it’s worse because I imagine the students or staff are seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about teaching mainly because of how you think you look, or is it also because you're worried about not being a good teacher in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly how I think I look. I mean… I do care about teaching well, but that’s not what’s getting stuck in my head. It’s seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and then thinking the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I’m here because I keep noticing my face in lesson videos. One side looks crooked to me, sort of swollen, and once I see it I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, even other staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your face that you're concerned about, or have you noticed anything else about your body that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. That one side… it looks crooked, kind of swollen on videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone’s looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance happen every time you see yourself in videos or is it something that comes and goes?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much whenever I see myself in those videos. Once I catch that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I can’t really unsee it. Then I start thinking everyone must be noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance bother you when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Mostly when other people might see it, yeah. Even if I’m alone watching the lesson video, once I notice that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I start thinking the students or parents or staff must be staring at it. It’s hard to get past that.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face's appearance affected your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, but I’ve fallen behind on a lot of things—lesson planning, mentoring, even basketball coaching stuff. I just don’t get things finished on time anymore.\n\nAdmin has started writing up missed deadlines and coverage issues, so… I’m pretty worried I might lose my position if it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen, or is it just that you feel like it appears that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it does look that way. Like one side of my face looks crooked, kind of swollen, especially on the lesson videos. And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. Or… how I see it, anyway. That crooked, swollen side on the videos — once I notice it, I just feel like they’re all staring at that instead of hearing what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people staring at you if you didn't notice the crooked or swollen appearance on the videos?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much. It really starts when I see it on the lesson videos… that crooked, swollen-looking side. Then I can’t get it out of my head, and I just assume everyone’s staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel just as anxious about teaching if you could somehow ensure that nobody noticed your face, but everything else about your teaching remained the same?\n\nPatient: I think… no, probably not just as anxious. If I somehow knew nobody could notice my face, the teaching itself might be okay. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I feel like the students, parents, staff… they’re all staring at that instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your face actually appears crooked or swollen to others when they look at you in person, not just in videos?\n\nPatient: I… I think it does, yes. Or at least I can’t shake that it does. When I see that crooked, swollen-looking side in the lesson videos, it feels like that must be what everyone sees in person too… students, parents, staff. Like they’re staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see your face as crooked or swollen when they look at you, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I think they do see it. I know maybe that sounds… I don’t know. But when I see that crooked, swollen side in the lesson videos, it feels like that’s what everyone must be looking at — the students, parents, staff — not what I’m actually saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that nobody else noticed or cared about the way your face looks?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. Even if no one noticed, I’d still see it. That crooked, swollen-looking side in the videos… I can’t stop focusing on it. But it’s worse because I imagine the students or staff are seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about teaching mainly because of how you think you look, or is it also because you're worried about not being a good teacher in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly how I think I look. I mean… I do care about teaching well, but that’s not what’s getting stuck in my head. It’s seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and then thinking the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides your face, that you worry others might be staring at or judging you for?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mostly my face. That crooked, swollen-looking side I keep seeing in the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone — students, parents, staff — must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry that people are staring at your face because of this specific appearance issue, or is it more that you feel like you're being judged as a person?\n\nPatient: It’s more the specific thing with my face. That crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I just… I feel like students or parents or staff must be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Once I notice it, I just… I feel like students or parents or staff must be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen when you look in a mirror, away from the videos?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / secondary_school_teacher / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the lesson videos, and once I notice it I feel like the students, parents, and staff must be staring at it instead of listening to anything I’m saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. One side looks crooked to me, sort of swollen, and once I see it I can’t stop thinking that the students, parents, even other staff are staring at that instead of listening to what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your face that you're concerned about, or have you noticed anything else about your body that bothers you?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face. That one side… it looks crooked, kind of swollen on videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone’s looking at that instead of what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance happen every time you see yourself in videos or is it something that comes and goes?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty much whenever I see myself in those videos. Once I catch that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I can’t really unsee it. Then I start thinking everyone must be noticing it too.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your face's appearance bother you when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Mostly when other people might see it, yeah. Even if I’m alone watching the lesson video, once I notice that side of my face looking crooked or swollen, I start thinking the students or parents or staff must be staring at it. It’s hard to get past that.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face's appearance affected your ability to teach or interact with your students and colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t really like admitting that, but I’ve fallen behind on a lot of things—lesson planning, mentoring, even basketball coaching stuff. I just don’t get things finished on time anymore.\n\nAdmin has started writing up missed deadlines and coverage issues, so… I’m pretty worried I might lose my position if it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen, or is it just that you feel like it appears that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it does look that way. Like one side of my face looks crooked, kind of swollen, especially on the lesson videos. And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. Or… how I see it, anyway. That crooked, swollen side on the videos — once I notice it, I just feel like they’re all staring at that instead of hearing what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people staring at you if you didn't notice the crooked or swollen appearance on the videos?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much. It really starts when I see it on the lesson videos… that crooked, swollen-looking side. Then I can’t get it out of my head, and I just assume everyone’s staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel just as anxious about teaching if you could somehow ensure that nobody noticed your face, but everything else about your teaching remained the same?\n\nPatient: I think… no, probably not just as anxious. If I somehow knew nobody could notice my face, the teaching itself might be okay. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I feel like the students, parents, staff… they’re all staring at that instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your face actually appears crooked or swollen to others when they look at you in person, not just in videos?\n\nPatient: I… I think it does, yes. I know maybe that sounds… I don’t know. But it’s worse because I imagine the students or staff are seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about teaching mainly because of how you think you look, or is it also because you're worried about not being a good teacher in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly how I think I look. I mean… I do care about teaching well, but that’s not what’s getting stuck in my head. It’s seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and then thinking the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides your face, that you worry others might be staring at or judging you for?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mostly my face. That crooked, swollen-looking side I keep seeing in the lesson videos. I don’t really like admitting that, but I’ve fallen behind on a lot of things—lesson planning, mentoring, even basketball coaching stuff. I just don’t get things finished on time anymore.\n\nAdmin has started writing up missed deadlines and coverage issues, so… I’m pretty worried I might lose my position if it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen, or is it just that you feel like it appears that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it does look that way. Like one side of my face looks crooked, kind of swollen, especially on the lesson videos. And once I see it, I can’t stop thinking that the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are staring at your face because of how it actually looks, or because you feel like you're not a good teacher or person?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of how it looks. Or… how I see it, anyway. That crooked, swollen side on the videos — once I notice it, I just feel like they’re all staring at that instead of hearing what I’m saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about people staring at you if you didn't notice the crooked or swollen appearance on the videos?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much. It really starts when I see it on the lesson videos… that crooked, swollen-looking side. Then I can’t get it out of my head, and I just assume everyone’s staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel just as anxious about teaching if you could somehow ensure that nobody noticed your face, but everything else about your teaching remained the same?\n\nPatient: I think… no, probably not just as anxious. If I somehow knew nobody could notice my face, the teaching itself might be okay. It’s when I see that crooked, swollen-looking side on the videos that I can’t stop thinking everyone’s staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the appearance of your face that you're worried about, or is it more about being judged or evaluated by others in general?\n\nPatient: It’s specifically my face. I mean… I don’t like being judged generally, but this is about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I feel like the students, parents, staff… they’re all staring at that instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your face actually appears crooked or swollen to others when they look at you in person, not just in videos?\n\nPatient: I… I think it does, yes. Or at least I can’t shake that it does. When I see that crooked, swollen-looking side in the lesson videos, it feels like that must be what everyone sees in person too… students, parents, staff. Like they’re staring at it instead of listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people actually see your face as crooked or swollen when they look at you, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I think they do see it. I know maybe that sounds… I don’t know. But when I see that crooked, swollen side in the lesson videos, it feels like that’s what everyone must be looking at — the students, parents, staff — not what I’m actually saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that nobody else noticed or cared about the way your face looks?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. Even if no one noticed, I’d still see it. That crooked, swollen-looking side in the videos… I can’t stop focusing on it. But it’s worse because I imagine the students or staff are seeing it too.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about teaching mainly because of how you think you look, or is it also because you're worried about not being a good teacher in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly how I think I look. I mean… I do care about teaching well, but that’s not what’s getting stuck in my head. It’s seeing that crooked, swollen-looking side of my face on the videos, and then thinking the students or parents or staff are staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides your face, that you worry others might be staring at or judging you for?\n\nPatient: Not really… it’s mostly my face. That crooked, swollen-looking side I keep seeing in the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone — students, parents, staff — must be staring at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry that people are staring at your face because of this specific appearance issue, or is it more that you feel like you're being judged as a person?\n\nPatient: It’s more the specific thing with my face. That crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the lesson videos. Once I notice it, I just… I feel like students or parents or staff must be looking at that instead of listening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked or swollen when you look in a mirror, away from the videos?\n\nPatient: In the mirror… sometimes I think I can see it, but it’s the videos that really make it obvious to me. Once I see that crooked, swollen-looking side, I can’t stop focusing on it. Then I just assume everyone else is staring at it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about teaching if your face looked perfectly normal to you, but you were being evaluated on your teaching performance?\n\nPatient: I mean… if my face looked normal to me, I don’t think it would be the same. The teaching evaluation would still matter, but this is more about that crooked, swollen-looking side I see on the videos. Once I notice it, I just feel like everyone’s staring at that, not really hearing what I’m saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },
